# GEORGE RIPLEY
## ENGLISH CANON
# THE BOOK OF THE TWELVE GATES.
## <note>original: "XII. Portarum Liber." This is the Latin translation of Ripley's famous alchemical poem "The Compound of Alchymie," which outlines twelve stages of the alchemical process.</note>

->*Rendered in Elegiac Verse*<-
<note>Elegiac verse is a classical poetic form consisting of couplets; the translator, Nicholas May, has adapted Ripley's original English verses into this formal Latin meter.</note>

->By<-

# NICHOLAS MAY.
<note>original: "Nicolao Maio." May was a 16th-century physician and poet known for translating alchemical works.</note>

->*The intellect judges the truth*<-

<summary>This is the title page of a Latin poetic adaptation of George Ripley’s "Twelve Gates," a seminal 15th-century alchemical text, translated here by Nicholas May.</summary>
<keywords>George Ripley, Nicholas May, Twelve Gates, alchemy, Latin poetry, title page, Bridlington Priory</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the manuscript to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II. The presence of the Vatican Library stamps ("BIBLIOTHECA APOSTOLICA VATICANA") confirms the provenance of this specific volume within the papal collections.</meta>

# TO THE MOST POWERFUL
## AND MOST INVINCIBLE PRIN-
ce and Lord, Lord **RUDOLF**
**II**, Emperor of the Romans, ever *August*,
King of Germany, Hungary, Bohemia, Dalmatia, etc.;
Archduke of Austria; Duke of Bur-
gundy, Silesia, etc.; Margrave
of Moravia, of both Lusatias <note>Upper and Lower Lusatia, regions in what is now eastern Germany and western Poland.</note>, etc.;
Count of Tyrol, etc. To his
most merciful Lord. Greetings. <note>original: "S.", shorthand for "Salutem," a traditional Latin salutation wishing health and prosperity.</note>

> Behold, my Muse brings forth Ripley <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was an influential English alchemist and canon of Bridlington; his works were highly prized at the imperial court.</note> speaking
> in verse: who will be his guardian, or who will be his patron?

<summary>This page contains a formal Latin dedication to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II, a famous patron of the occult and alchemy, presenting the work of George Ripley under his protection.</summary>
<keywords>Rudolf II, George Ripley, Holy Roman Empire, alchemy, patronage, dedication, titles of nobility</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Emperor Rudolf II, transitioning from the introductory titles to a poetic defense of alchemy and a personal appeal for the Emperor's patronage. It establishes Rudolf as a unique and enlightened protector of the "Sacred Art" in an age of skepticism.</meta>

**Our** age is indeed not unlearned; yet it is
    Not quite fair enough in its prejudices.
**This** man, condemning the most learned writings of the ancient Sages <note>original: "Sophorum," referring to the wise men or philosophers of antiquity, particularly those associated with the hermetic arts.</note>
    When he achieves nothing, calls them "barbarous writings";
**That** man despises the Art of Alchemy <note>original: "Cymiae," an archaic term encompassing both chemistry and alchemy.</note> as if it were false,
    And thinks the books of Ripley <gloss>and</gloss> <note>George Ripley, the 15th-century English alchemist whose work this manuscript presents.</note> are but empty dreams.
**You**, however, out of many—O Emperor Rudolf—perhaps you alone
    Approve the harmonious writings of the ancients regarding Alchemy.
**You** do not see these things as false, but as gifts from highest God <note>original: "Iovæ" (Jove). In Renaissance Latin poetry, the name of the king of the Roman gods was frequently used to refer to the Christian God.</note>,
    In which He Himself rejoices to be recognized; these You cherish.
**Therefore**, as a suppliant seeking a protector, O greatest Emperor,
    From among many thousands, my Muse calls upon You.

<summary>A Latin poem dedicated to Rudolf II, praising his unique wisdom in recognizing alchemy as a divine gift rather than a "barbarous" or "false" art, unlike his prejudiced contemporaries.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Rudolf II, George Ripley, patronage, Renaissance science, divine gift, Sages</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory dedication to Emperor Rudolf II. While the previous page defended the "Sacred Art" of alchemy against skeptics, this page provides the specific reasons for the work’s presentation, naming the influential alchemist Edward Kelley as the catalyst for the project. A large circular stamp from the "Biblioteca Apostolica Vaticana" (Vatican Apostolic Library) is centered on the page, partially obscuring the text.</meta>

My reason is twofold: the first is that you understand the **Arts**,
You favor **Philosophy** <note>In this context, "Philosophy" specifically refers to Natural Philosophy and Alchemical wisdom.</note>, and you cherish those who practice them.
The other is that **Kelley** <note>Edward Kelley (1555–1597), the famous English alchemist and medium who was a central figure at Rudolf II’s court in Prague.</note>, more excellent than any other,
Joyfully commanded me to weave this work here in verse.
Continue, O **Caesar** <note>A traditional title for the Holy Roman Emperor, in this case, Rudolf II.</note>, to protect the good **Arts** even under the shadow of **Mars** <note>original: "sub Marte." This refers to the god of war, suggesting that even during times of military conflict, the Emperor should maintain his patronage of science and art.</note>,
And as a good man yourself, continue to cherish good craftsmen.
Thus you shall bear the palm of victory from **Mars** and the laurel from **Apollo** <note>The Greek and Roman god of music, poetry, and light; the laurel crown symbolizes intellectual or artistic triumph.</note>;
So that you may have a twofold honor: that of **War** and that of **Art**.

## To Your Sacred Imperial Majesty

->Your most humble servant,<-

### Nikolaus Mai <note>Nikolaus Mai (1550–1615) was a humanist poet, lawyer, and diplomat who served as a counselor to Emperor Rudolf II.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the poetic dedication to Emperor Rudolf II, citing the Emperor's own wisdom and the personal recommendation of the alchemist Edward Kelley as the reasons for the book's creation. It petitions the Emperor to continue supporting the arts and sciences despite the pressures of war.</summary>
<keywords>Edward Kelley, Nikolaus Mai, Rudolf II, patronage, Mars and Apollo, alchemy, Holy Roman Empire, Vatican Library</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to Emperor Rudolf II by Nikolaus Mai to a shorter, introductory poem by a figure named Helleus. It addresses the reader directly, pivoting from the previous page's focus on Edward Kelley’s influence to the scholarly merit of the translation itself. The OCR notes significant bleed-through and a modern watermark, which may have hindered the initial transcription of the heading.</meta>

<unclear>Helleus to the reader</unclear>

# Helleus <insert>TO</insert> the Reader :

> Whatever the Assembly of Philosophers <note>original: "Turba philosophorum." This likely refers to the *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the most ancient and influential alchemical texts, or more generally to the collective wisdom of the ancient sages.</note> had gathered in order,
> the native tongue of **Ripley** <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was a famous English alchemist and canon of Bridlington whose works were originally written in Middle English verse.</note> gave forth in song.
> These same things **Maier** <note>Michael Maier (1568–1622), the celebrated physician and alchemist, translated Ripley’s English verses into Latin to make them accessible to the international Republic of Letters.</note> makes to be Latin with his pen;
> Hence **Ripley** is known, but that man [Maier] is even more renowned.

<summary>A brief commendatory poem by Helleus praising Michael Maier for translating the English alchemical verses of George Ripley into Latin, thereby increasing the fame of both men.</summary>
<keywords>Helleus, George Ripley, Michael Maier, alchemy, translation, Latin, Turba Philosophorum, English alchemical poetry</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page follows a short commendatory poem by Helleus. It transitions to a defensive and defiant poem by Nikolaus Mai (N.M.), the author/editor, who anticipates and dismisses criticism of his work. The focus shifts from the pedigree of the translation (Ripley and Maier) to the philosophical substance of the "chemical" verses themselves.</meta>

# To Momus.
<note>In Greek mythology, Momus was the personification of satire, mockery, and unfair criticism. In Renaissance literature, authors frequently addressed "Momus" in their prefaces to ward off critics who might disparage their work without understanding it.</note>

I do not deny that Momus will look down upon my chemical verses,
->And will say they contain none of the pure honey of eloquence.<-
Indeed, I desire this very thing, which the Muse requested long ago:
->To have displeased the ignorant with a chemical book.<-
<note>original: "Chymico insipidis displicuisse libro." The author suggests that if "insipid" or witless people dislike the book, it is a sign of the book's specialized value.</note>
Let it be enough for me to have found favor with the good and those skilled in the Art;
->Those for whom these things hold no taste can do no harm.<-
Though my chemical verses may seem foolish to you, Momus,
->Nonetheless, I know that the words did not lack for substance.<-
<note>original: "Verba tamen rebus non caruisse scio." A common defense in technical writing: the style may be plain, but the "rebus" (the matter or facts of alchemy) are solid.</note>
An "insipid" work, to a man for whom the proven writings of the Sages <term>Sophorum</term> <note>The "Sages" (Latin: *Sophi*) refers to the legendary masters of alchemy, such as Hermes Trismegistus, whose hidden wisdom was the foundation of the craft.</note>
->Cannot be wise, no matter how much wisdom they hold!<-

->N.M.<-
<note>These initials refer to Nikolaus Mai (Nicolaus Maius), a poet and counselor to Emperor Rudolf II, who was deeply involved in the alchemical circles of the time.</note>

<summary>A defiant poem by Nikolaus Mai addressing Momus, the god of mockery. Mai defends his alchemical verses, stating that while they may lack poetic "honey," they contain true substance intended only for those skilled in the alchemical art.</summary>
<keywords>Momus, Nikolaus Mai, alchemy, alchemical poetry, Sages, criticism, Chemical Art, Sophorum</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the severely damaged and fragmentary fragments of the preceding leaf to a clearly legible section. The handwriting is a crisp humanist cursive, and the text itself is a well-known didactic poem by the English alchemist George Ripley. This marks the beginning of the instructional portion of the manuscript, shifting from defensive or introductory remarks to the core philosophical and moral requirements of the "Great Work."</meta>

# RIPLEY’S PROLOGUE.

My son, faithfully apply your ears to my warnings,
Receiving my golden sayings through this teaching.
And let wisdom establish her seat within your heart,
She who was truly loved by the Sages of old. <note>The "Sages" (Sophis) refers to the ancient masters of alchemy and philosophy who sought the Philosopher's Stone.</note>
Be pure in soul: do not sin: lay aside 
Your proud arrogance; may you bear a gentle heart.
Unless you do this; unless you are humble; the Most High from on high
Will show you the pleasing gifts of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophia"</note> far less.
He who contemplates impious things in a wicked heart, lacks the breath 
Of the sacred Divinity, and is deprived of His gifts. <term>Divinity</term> <note>The Latin "Numen" refers to a divine power or presiding spirit, often used in alchemical texts to describe the spiritual force behind natural transformations.</note>
The holy Divinity does not dwell in a wicked heart;
Therefore, fearing God, may you observe His sacred commands.

<summary>This page contains the opening of George Ripley’s prologue, a poem in Latin elegiac couplets that serves as a moral exhortation to the aspiring alchemist. It emphasizes that spiritual purity, humility, and the fear of God are essential prerequisites for receiving the "golden" secrets of ancient wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>George Ripley, alchemy, moral preparation, Sophia, divine wisdom, didactic poetry, humility, Numen</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the verse prologue of George Ripley, maintaining the humanist cursive hand and the moralizing tone established on the previous leaf. The watermark mentioned in the OCR refers to the archival collection where the manuscript is held.</meta>

For God, as I have seen, never abandons the just;
Nor have those who worshipped God perished from hunger.
Ensure that Prudence <note>In an alchemical context, Prudence refers to the careful, measured approach required for the Great Work, avoiding haste or greed.</note> becomes your friend through your mutual love;
And you shall say to Wisdom <note>original: "Sophia"</note>: "You are my dear sister."
She herself will guide your steps along a straight path,
Lest a wicked error drive you far from the truth.
Love will join you as a companion, as will Integrity <note>original: "Honestas"</note>,
Virtues which ensure you may be pleasing to all.
Therefore, so that you may be wise and prudent in both heart and speech,
Your prayers shall grant you this through a divine gift.
Surrounded by these virtues, every kind of blessing
Will raise you to the stars <note>original: "astra." To be "raised to the stars" signifies the successful completion of the alchemical process and the attainment of spiritual and physical perfection.</note> by an exalted step.

<term>God</term> <note>original: "Deus"</note>, <term>Prudence</term> <note>original: "Prudentia"</note>, <term>Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Sophia"</note>, <term>Integrity</term> <note>original: "Honestas"</note>, <term>Virtues</term> <note>original: "virtutibus"</note>, <term>Stars</term> <note>original: "astra"</note>

<summary>This page concludes the moral exhortation of Ripley’s prologue, framing the alchemical pursuit as a journey guided by personified virtues like Prudence and Wisdom. It promises that the practitioner who maintains spiritual integrity will eventually reach the "stars," a metaphor for alchemical success and enlightenment.</summary>

<keywords>George Ripley, Prudence, Sophia, Wisdom, Integrity, divine gift, spiritual alchemy, moral preparation, Great Work</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the verse prologue attributed to George Ripley. While the previous page established the moral requirements of the alchemist—Prudence and Integrity—this page focuses on the rewards of the "learned art," contrasting material wealth with the spiritual and intellectual riches of Wisdom (Sophia).</meta>

These riches, which you have prepared by learned art <note>The "learned art" refers to alchemy, which the author frames as a disciplined, scholarly pursuit rather than mere luck or greed.</note>,
Will not disturb your heart, nor will they bring you painful losses.
Instead, they will grant you renown with a great reward <note>original: "foenore." In a financial sense, this means interest or profit, but here it refers to the spiritual return on the alchemist's labor.</note>,
So that you might avoid evils and choose a better path.
For renowned Virtue will bring all these good deeds to you:
Because these are the rare treasures of the Goddess Wisdom <note>original: "Sophiae... Deae." Sophia is the Greek word for Wisdom, often personified in alchemical texts as a divine guide or bride to the philosopher.</note>.
Wisdom is beyond compare; she surpasses
The treasures of the world and the riches of the Tagus <note>The Tagus is a river in Spain and Portugal that was famous in the ancient world for the gold found in its sands.</note>.
Truly, whatever is considered precious on earth,
When compared to Wisdom, is said to be but mud.
Virtue will bring infinite gifts to its practitioner,
So that ~~in~~ <insert>he</insert> may be rich in heaven, and also rich upon the earth.

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| <term>riches</term> | <note>original: "divitiae"</note> The material and spiritual results of the alchemical process. |
| <term>Wisdom</term> | <note>original: "Sophia"</note> Divine wisdom personified. |
| <term>wisdom</term> | <note>original: "sapientia"</note> The human capacity for understanding and discernment. |
| <term>virtue</term> | <note>original: "virtus"</note> Moral excellence and the inherent "power" or "potency" of a substance. |
| <term>Tagus</term> | <note>original: "Tagus"</note> A river synonymous with natural, unrefined gold. |
| <term>reward</term> | <note>original: "foenus"</note> The profit or "interest" earned through spiritual and physical labor. |

<summary>This page praises the spiritual and material rewards of alchemy, asserting that the wisdom gained through the "learned art" far surpasses earthly wealth like the gold of the Tagus river. It concludes the prologue by promising that the virtuous alchemist will find prosperity both in the divine and earthly realms.</summary>

<keywords>Wisdom, Sophia, Virtue, Tagus, alchemical riches, George Ripley, spiritual alchemy, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the verse prologue. While the previous page celebrated the infinite rewards and "infinite gifts" of Wisdom (Sophia), this page transitions into a moral warning. It suggests that while the alchemical "medicine" provides wealth and power, these gifts are fragile and can be lost through sin, just as famous Biblical figures lost their divine favor.</meta>

Pray that God may grant these holy gifts to you.
They are the medicine for your body and your soul.
They will make you rich in wealth and powerful in titles,
So that you may find favor with great princes.
Ponder in your mind for what reason Adam lost his endowments <note>original: "dotes." This refers to the "Original Justice" or the state of grace Adam possessed before the Fall.</note>,
And Samson his strength.
Do you see how Saul was stripped of his kingdom through his crimes,
And David was punished for his debaucheries? <note>original: "stupra." This refers to the Biblical King David’s adultery with Bathsheba and the moral failings that led to turmoil in his house.</note>
Do you see how that most handsome of men <note>The author refers to Absalom, David's son, who was famous for his beauty but died when his long hair became caught in the branches of an oak tree during a battle.</note>
Hung himself in an oak tree by his own hair?
Do you see Hezekiah, afflicted with disease because of his sins,
And how many others have perished in a similar way?

But on the contrary <note>original: "At contra." This is the catchword, intended to lead into the next page's contrast of virtuous behavior.</note>

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| <term>Adam</term> | <note>original: "Adamus"</note> The first man, who lost his divine gifts through disobedience. |
| <term>Samson</term> | <note>original: "Samson"</note> The biblical judge who lost his strength when his secret was betrayed. |
| <term>Saul</term> | <note>original: "Saulus"</note> The first king of Israel, who lost God's favor and his throne. |
| <term>David</term> | <note>original: "Dauid"</note> The king who suffered for his moral failings. |
| <term>Hezekiah</term> | <note>original: "Ezechiam"</note> A king of Judah who was struck with illness as a trial or punishment. |
| <term>perished</term> | <note>original: "interijsse"</note> To have died, passed away, or been destroyed. |
| <term>medicine</term> | <note>original: "medicina"</note> A remedy or cure; in an alchemical context, this refers to the Elixir’s power to heal the body and "perfect" the soul. |

# OF GEORGE RIPLEY, CANON
## THE ENGLISHMAN
### To his book of the Twelve Gates
### PREFACE.

> O God, O Light to be revered with majesty,
> Who can be understood by no squaring <note>original: "quadratione." In an alchemical context, this likely refers to the impossibility of defining the Divine through human geometry or logic, potentially alluding to the "squaring of the circle"—a famous mathematical and alchemical problem.</note>,
> A brilliance shrouding our sight with its rays,
> Triple in Persons, O God, and a Unity <note>original: "Ente monas." The word "monas" is a Greek-derived term meaning an indivisible unit or "monad." Here, Ripley uses it to describe the oneness of God's essence despite the three persons of the Trinity.</note> in Being.
> Purifier of souls, O first eternity <note>original: "perpetuatio prima."</note>,
> O God, O both the light and glory of my soul:
> We beseech You: having lifted us from this valley of the world, may You, kind
> And full of mercy, place us above the stars.

    that these matters are described with less clarity in other writings.

    I believe there are three sacred <term>Mercuries of the Art <note>original: "Mercurios Artis"; in alchemy, "Mercury" does not always mean the liquid metal quicksilver, but rather the transformative "spirit" or "solvent" extracted from various substances.</note></term>.
I would rightly call these the certain and divine keys
    of the Art: those which you call the "threefold menstrua" <note>original: "menstrua trina"; in alchemical terminology, a "menstruum" is a solvent capable of dissolving solids or extracting their essence.</note> of <term>Lully <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1315), a famous philosopher and polymath. Many influential alchemical texts were attributed to him, emphasizing the use of specialized solvents or "menstruums."</note></term>.
Without these, we know that nothing can be done correctly; without
    these three, you are merely throwing seeds into sterile fields.
Two of these are those which we see manifest,
    and which have the name and the appearance of the "surface."
The third is the essence of the <term>Sun <note>Gold</note></term> and <term>Moon <note>Silver</note></term>;
    its properties shall be made known through my song.

<summary>The author identifies three "sacred Mercuries" or "menstruums" as the essential keys to alchemy, citing the authority of Ramon Lull. He explains that two are external or visible, while the third is the internal essence of gold and silver.</summary>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the previously illegible leaf to a formal listing of the twelve stages of the alchemical process. These "gates" are a central theme in the works of George Ripley, a famous 15th-century English alchemist whose writings this manuscript appears to summarize or reference.</meta>

# THE TWELVE GATES <note>original: "XII PORTÆ"; this title refers to the essential stages of the "Great Work" in alchemy, traditionally associated with the English alchemist George Ripley.</note>

1. **Calcination** <note>original: "Calcinatio"; the process of heating a substance until it is reduced to a fine powder or ash.</note>
2. **Solution** <note>original: "Solútio"; the dissolving of the solid matter into a liquid state.</note>
3. **Separation** <note>original: "Separatio"; the filtering or isolating of the pure parts of the matter from the impure.</note>
4. **Conjunction** <note>original: "Coniunctio"; the reuniting of the separated elements into a new, unified form, often called the "chemical wedding."</note>
5. **Putrefaction** <note>original: "Putrefactio"; a stage of decay or "blackening" where the substance dies to its old form to be reborn.</note>
6. **Congelation** <note>original: "Congelatio"; the thickening or solidification of the liquid matter.</note>
7. **Cibation** <note>original: "Cibatio"; literally "feeding," where the matter is nourished with liquid to sustain its growth.</note>
8. **Sublimation** <note>original: "Súblimatio"; the process of heating a substance so it rises as vapor and solidifies again at the top of the vessel.</note>
9. **Fermentation** <note>original: "Fermentatio"; adding a "ferment" to the substance to give it a specific quality, much like yeast in bread.</note>
10. **Exaltation** <note>original: "Exaltatio"; the refinement of the substance to a higher, more potent state of being.</note>
11. **Multiplication** <note>original: "Múltiplicatio"; increasing the power, quantity, or effectiveness of the alchemical medicine.</note>
12. **Projection** <note>original: "Proiectio"; the final step where the finished "Philosopher's Stone" is cast upon base metals to transform them into gold.</note>

<term>Alchemy</term> <note>A philosophical and protoscientific tradition aimed at the transformation of matter and the attainment of spiritual perfection.</note>, <term>George Ripley</term> <note>A prominent 15th-century Augustinian canon and alchemist whose work "The Compound of Alchymy" popularized the system of the Twelve Gates.</note>

<summary>This page lists the Twelve Gates of Alchemy, a systematic series of chemical and spiritual stages required to complete the "Great Work" or create the Philosopher's Stone. It serves as a structural table of contents for the processes described in the preceding poetic sections of the manuscript.</summary>
<keywords>Twelve Gates, George Ripley, Great Work, alchemical stages, transformation, Philosopher's Stone</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page begins the detailed explanation of the first stage, Calcination, continuing from the list of the twelve gates provided on the previous page. The text is written in verse, common for alchemical instructions of this period, to make the steps easier to memorize.</meta>

## 1
# ON CALCINATION <note>original: "DE CALCINATIONE"; this is the first of the twelve stages or "gates" of the alchemical process.</note>

If you are able to turn the **Stone** <note>original: "Lapidem"; the "Philosopher's Stone" in its early, raw state.</note> into a fine powder,
You strip away all impurities and cleanse it of its faults.
Conversely, if you remove its harmful contaminations,
Through purification, that **Stone** will become a fine ash <note>original: "calx"</note>.
Once the impurities are wiped away, **Nature**—using its own innate heat—
Restores the dead parts of its **Stone**.
Indeed, she takes nothing away from its natural vigor,
But dissolves it just as the work requires.
Therefore, study the reliable footprints of the **Sages** <note>original: "Sophorum"</note>,
Lest your work be carried out in the crude manner of the common people.

**Calcination** <term>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a fine powder or "calx."</term>, **Stone** <term>Lapis: the central material used in the alchemical transformation.</term>, **Impurities** <term>Sordibus: the "dross" or waste material that must be removed from the raw matter.</term>, **Nature**, **Heat**, **Sages** <term>Sophorum: the "Wise Ones" or master alchemists whose methods must be followed.</term>, **Ash** <term>Calx: the chemical result of calcination, a fine, dry powder.</term>

<summary>This page describes the first alchemical stage, Calcination, emphasizing the need to reduce the matter to a fine powder to remove impurities. It portrays Nature as the primary agent of restoration and warns the practitioner to follow the methods of the masters rather than common, unrefined techniques.</summary>
<keywords>Calcination, Philosopher's Stone, purification, Nature, Sages, alchemical process, chemical transformation</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---

A wise man recognizes these **ashes** <term>calces: the "calx" or fine powder produced by the process of calcination, which the alchemist seeks to refine without destroying its essence.</term> the least, and holds them not,

->That the vital riches of the **ash** are not diminished.<-

->Join **kind to its own kind** <note>original: "Junge genus generi"; a fundamental alchemical maxim stating that transformation must happen within the same family of substances—gold is made from gold, not from "foreign" or unrelated materials.</note>; and do not choose foreign things.<-

->And certain **seeds** <term>semina: the concentrated essence of a substance that allows it to reproduce its own form.</term> bear similar fruits.<-
Man begets man: the beast brings forth living
->Beasts: every **herb** fosters its own species.<-

Finally, every **spirit** <term>spiritus: the volatile essence or "breath" of a substance that tends to evaporate when heated.</term> is well **fixed** <note>original: "figitur"; fixation is the alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to heat so it no longer evaporates.</note> by an **ash** <term>calx: the fine powder or "lime" produced by calcination; the alchemist views this as the physical body that will house the spirit.</term> of its own kind.

For they take the span of a year, and the **ash** <term>calx: the fine powder produced by the process of calcination, representing the "body" of the work.</term> to be perfected will arrive in more or less time.

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for mixing the metallic components—Gold, Silver, and Mercury—into the alchemical "paste." While the previous page established a general ratio, this page cites the authorities of Raymond Lully and Roger Bacon to offer nuanced variations in the proportions of the "body" and "spirit."</meta>

Then, let our **Sun** <term>Sol: the alchemical name for Gold.</term> receive four parts of this **Mercury** <term>Mercurius: the "Spirit" or philosophical quicksilver used to dissolve the metals.</term>, and our **Cynthia** <note>original: "Cynthia"; a poetic name for the Moon, which represents Silver in the alchemical tradition.</note> two.

Begin our work according to this logic: let the **body** <note>The "body" (corpus) refers to the solid, fixed metallic component of the mixture.</note> receive three parts, and the **Spirit** <note>The "spirit" (spiritus) refers to the volatile or liquid component.</note> just as many.

These ratios are true, just as **Raymond** <note>Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and major authority in alchemical literature whose works focused on the transmutation of metals.</note> discovered; my Master makes these things manifest to me through practice.

But **Bacon** <note>Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292), an English philosopher and friar known for his "Opus Majus" and his influential writings on the experimental nature of alchemy.</note> took three parts of spirits for one part of the body, as his eloquent writings teach.

I spent sleepless nights and days over these matters before I could truly understand them.

You may choose whichever recipe you wish: each author has told you the truth, and both are consistent in their own writings.

<summary>This page provides specific mathematical ratios for combining Gold, Silver, and Mercury, contrasting the different proportional methods advocated by the legendary alchemists Raymond Lully and Roger Bacon.</summary>

These shall endure in a perpetual fire; you may bring back to a new life that which had perished in death. 

See to it that, at the same time, the **three** <note>The "three-in-one" (trinúm... unúm) refers to the unification of the *Tria Prima*—Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury—into a single, harmonious substance.</note> become one without conflict. This is the best method of calculation.

### Key Terms of the Work
**Clay** <note>original: "argilla"</note>: The raw, workable matter of the stone.
**Potter** <note>original: "figulus"</note>: A metaphor for the alchemist who must "shape" the elements.
**Lime** <note>original: "calx"</note>: The result of calcination, or heating to a powder.
**Moisture/Humor** <note>original: "humor"</note>: The liquid or volatile component of the mixture.
**The Moon** <note>original: "Luna"</note>: The principle of cold and moisture.
**Phoebus (The Sun)** <note>original: "Phoebus"</note>: The principle of heat and dryness.
**Natures** <note>original: "naturae"</note>: The elemental building blocks of reality.
**Alchemy** <note>original: "alchimia"</note>: The art of transmutation and perfection.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the *nigredo* stage from page 25, explaining the metaphorical "death" of the spirit as it descends into the dark matter. Indeed, the **Spirit** is sent down from the sustenance of the earth to the shadows,
    Like one who drinks **deadly poisons** <term>toxica dira: This refers to the toxic or volatile vapors released during the initial decomposition of the materials within the alchemical vessel.</term>.
To this substance, a variety of names derived from dark things is given,
    Which remains until it begins to turn **bright white**.
But once the **blackness** <term>nigredo: The first major stage of the alchemical process, representing putrefaction and the "death" of the base metal before its transformation.</term> is stripped away and the **body** shines,
    Then it is accustomed to hold a more pleasant name.
Having obtained **shining white names** <term>albedo: The second stage of the work, symbolizing purification and the washing away of impurities to reach a state of silver-like clarity.</term>, it glows white; at last,
    When it turns **red**, it takes its names from red things <term>rubedo: The final stage of the "Great Work," where the matter is "perfected" into the red Philosopher's Stone, capable of transmuting metals.</term>.
You now enter the first thresholds of the first **Gate of the Citadel of Phoebus** <note>original: "Portæ Phœbi Arcis"; Phoebus Apollo is the Greco-Roman god of the Sun. In alchemy, the Sun represents Gold and the ultimate spiritual illumination.</note>,
    Which is inhabited only by the **Sages** <term>Sophis: From the Greek *sophoi*, referring to the "Wise Men" or true alchemists who have mastered the secrets of nature.</term>.
Plant your foot prudently; thus the door and the remaining gates
    Will perhaps soon be **unlocked** for you.

# ON DISSOLUTION.

Now I shall say a few words on what a genuine **dissolution** <term>solutio: the process of turning a solid substance into a liquid state, often seen as the "death" of the material before its rebirth.</term> is:
This was previously hidden, but now has been made manifest.
These now refine what were once dense in body;
The **first solvents** <term>Menstrua: Alchemical solvents, so named because they were believed to nourish the "embryo" of the philosopher's stone, much like biological cycles.</term> accomplish this by their own powers.
In these solvents, bodies suffer an **eclipse** <note>original: "Eclipsin"; a metaphor for the "nigredo" or blackening stage, where the matter loses its light and form.</note>,
And having been bound by their own chains, they now lie open and unlocked.
And they return to their **first matter** <term>Materia: the "Materia Prima" or the chaotic, formless base substance from which all things originate.</term> by the gift of Nature,
And they seek out again their first seeds.
These are one in kind; they are many in number, though not from one alone:
The **Sun** <note>original: "Sol"; representing the masculine principle, or the metal Gold.</note> is the father, and the **Moon** <note>original: "Luna"; representing the feminine principle, or the metal Silver.</note> takes the name of the mother.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical stage of dissolution, where solid matter is broken down by "solvents" into its primal state. It characterizes this transformation as an eclipse and identifies the Sun and Moon as the fundamental parent principles of the work.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on Dissolution from page 05. Having established that the "Sun" and "Moon" (Gold and Silver) are the parents of the work, the author now defines the specific substances and the "mediator" required to unify them into a liquid state.</meta>

Here is **Mercury** <term>Mercurius: specifically "Philosophical Mercury," the fluid principle believed to bridge the gap between the physical body and the spiritual essence.</term>, the one and only mediator;
These three suffice; do not seek more for yourself. <note>The "three" typically refers to the alchemical triad of Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt, or the Father, Mother, and Offspring mentioned in the following lines.</note>
These three are our **Adrop** <term>Adrop: a cryptic term used by alchemists to refer to their "Lead" or the raw, unrefined mineral body from which the work begins.</term>, our **Magnesia** <term>Magnesia: not the modern element magnesium, but a mystical name for the "mother" or the receptive, feminine matter of the Stone.</term>:
They are the Father and the Mother: he is the Brother, she the Sister.
This one acts, and that one is acted upon <note>original: "hic agit, haec patitur"; this describes the relationship between the active masculine (Sulfur) and passive feminine (Mercury) principles.</note>: they are one in a single essence,
**Mercury** and **Sulfur** <term>Sulphur: the active, fiery principle that provides form, heat, and "soul" to the substance.</term>; there is no need for anything more.

A medium grows up, which you shall join together by your art.
This is our Mercury, which I call the **Solvent** <term>Menstrua: the "Menstruum" or universal solvent capable of reducing metals to their first liquid state.</term>:
And Sulfur, whose spirit acts within
Our bodies, because more violently than the fires of hell
It burns them away, until they become **mineral water** <term>mineralis aqua: the final product of dissolution; a metallic liquid that holds the "seed" for the next stage of the work.</term>.

In this way, the bodies are rightly dissolved into the first water,

A new form may arise through a new birth.

Which is said to be the single **foundation** <term>basis: the fundamental principle or starting point of the Great Work.</term> of all secrets.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;if this is ignored, the **Work** <term>opus: the "Magnum Opus" or Great Work of creating the Stone.</term> will fail of its own accord.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;than you could endure with your own bare hand.
Do not change this fire during the entire time; but let it be such
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;that you can place your hand upon it and remove it whenever you like.

### III
# CONCERNING SEPARATION

It separates the dense from the thin, and divides the
Subtle from the thick; this now comes as the **Third Gate**. <note>original: "Tertia Porta." In George Ripley’s system, Separation follows Calcination and Solution. It involves distilling the essence from the dross.</note>
Such a division is not a work of the hands: <note>The author emphasizes that this is a natural, internal chemical process, not a mechanical filtering or manual picking apart of ingredients.</note> let the foolish
Expect no benefits for themselves from such [manual labor].
Here nature never ceases while she separates; by this means
Our **Elements** <term>elementa: the four classical elements—earth, water, air, and fire—which the alchemist seeks to purify and balance.</term> may reach the fifth degree. <note>This refers to the "Quintessence" or "Fifth Essence," the purest state of matter that transcends the four physical elements.</note>
The **Earth** <term>tellus: in this context, the solid, "fixed" matter at the bottom of the vessel, typically associated with the color black or the stage of "Nigredo."</term>, black in its own color, turns itself into water:
This will be air, when the white color shines forth.
Finally, if the **Stone** <term>lapis: the "Philosopher's Stone," the final perfected substance of the alchemical work.</term> should grow red, the air passes into fire—
God has given no more elements than these to things.

<summary>This page describes the stage of Separation, where the alchemical matter is refined through a natural, internal process rather than manual labor. It details the transformation of the four elements through a sequence of color changes—from black earth to white air and finally to red fire—aiming for the perfection of the "fifth degree."</summary>
<keywords>Separation, Third Gate, Four Elements, Quintessence, Nigredo, Alchemical transformation, George Ripley, color stages</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the verse explanation of the Third Gate, "Separation," in George Ripley’s alchemical system. It transitions from the physical description of the elements changing colors to the philosophical and biblical justification for the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone.</meta>

From there, our much-desired **Stone** <term>Lapis: the Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy, capable of perfecting both metals and the human body.</term> is created through the art,
Which the **Sages** <term>Sophi: the wise philosophers and masters of alchemy who preceded the author.</term> seek with such great diligence.
But there are more things for me to say in verse: by what means
And in what way our work of art would reach its completion.
This fourfold dispersal of the **Elements** <note>original: "Elementorum." This refers to the breaking down of matter into its fundamental components—earth, air, fire, and water—so they can be purified and recombined.</note> is spoken of
By the Philosophers, just as these ancient writings teach.
This same process is found depicted in a similar manner
In your Psalms, O great Prophet **David**. <note>The author is referring to King David, traditionally considered the author of the Psalms. Alchemists often cited the Bible to prove that their "Art" was a divine science practiced by holy men.</note>
For God led the purest rivers out of the rocks,
And gave the pleasing gifts of oil from the stones. <note>This alludes to biblical imagery (such as Psalm 78:16 or Deuteronomy 32:13) where God provides water and oil from flinty rocks, used here as a metaphor for extracting the precious alchemical essence from solid matter.</note>
Thus the stone is an oil that is never consumed by fire,
And, if you are wise, it gives you waters in abundance. <note>The "incombustible oil" and the "living water" are common alchemical metaphors for the stabilized, perfected substance of the Stone.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the Third Gate (Separation) by linking the alchemical process to biblical miracles. <meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of "Connivance" or "Gate" imagery into the fourth major stage of the alchemical Great Work: Conjunction. It marks the shift from the analytical labor of separating the elements to the synthetic labor of uniting them.</meta>

# IV
# ON CONJUNCTION.

Thus far it has been told, with what labor
The Elements of the sacred Stone <note>original: "Lapidis... sacri." The "sacred Stone" refers to the Philosopher’s Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy. The text notes that before they can be joined, the elements must first be painstakingly separated or purified.</note> are to be separated.
Now follows the conjunction of the Elements, which
Contains many secret instructions for the matter.
It joins clashing natures <note>original: "repugnantes naturas." This refers to the fundamental alchemical concept of the *coincidentia oppositorum*—the union of opposites, such as the "Fixed" and the "Volatile," or "Sulfur" and "Mercury."</note> into one,
And so transforms them, that none may prepare to flee. <note>original: "fugam." In a laboratory context, "fleeing" refers to volatility or evaporation. The goal of Conjunction is to "fix" the substance so it can withstand heat without vanishing as vapor.</note>
Especially when they undergo the trial in the fire,
The firm bonds of their marriage <note>original: "coniugij." Alchemical union was frequently depicted as a "Chemical Wedding" between a King and Queen, symbolizing the perfect integration of masculine and feminine principles.</note> remain.
Hence, for the learned, conjunction is nothing other than that which
Connects distinct things, and binds the principle <gloss>and binds the beginning</gloss>.

<summary>This page introduces the fourth stage of the Great Work, Conjunction, which focuses on uniting the previously separated elements into a stable, unified whole. It emphasizes the "Chemical Wedding" of opposing natures and their ability to remain joined even when tested by fire.</summary>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on "Conjunction," specifically addressing the necessity of the "middle way" in elemental balance and introducing the specific types of union recognized by alchemical tradition. It follows the previous page's discussion on separating the Elements before attempting their final bond.</meta>

Unless someone first separates them according to the Order of Nature, <note>original: "Ordine Naturæ." Alchemists believed that to reach a pure state, the chaotic "massa confusa" of raw matter had to be broken down into its elemental constituents following natural laws before they could be perfectly recombined.</note>
They cannot join the Elements <note>original: "Elementa." The four basic components—Earth, Water, Air, and Fire—which must be purified individually.</note> of their own accord.
Therefore, be steadfast, and let no error drive you from the right path: <note>original: "tramite recto." A common metaphor in hermetic literature for the specific, disciplined sequence of operations required to succeed.</note>

See that you imitate the potters <note>original: "plastas." Literally those who mold or shape materials like clay or wax.</note> who know how to fashion the Clay, <note>original: "Lutum." While this can refer to the "lute" used to seal vessels, here it is a metaphor for the alchemical matter itself, which requires a specific consistency to be workable.</note>
Ensuring it is neither too dry nor too diluted.
In this way, you will dry your "water" <note>original: "undam." Literally "wave" or "surge," referring here to the volatile moisture or "phlegm" in the chemical mixture that must be slowly evaporated.</note> more safely,
Provided you keep to the middle way that I show you.
Now, Conjunction <note>original: "coniunctio." The fourth stage of the Great Work, where the separated and purified parts are reunited.</note> is said to be of three kinds,
Among which the first is called "double" by the Sages. <note>original: "Sophis." The "Wise" or the "Philosophers," referring to the lineage of alchemical authorities.</note>
This consists of a relationship between the active <note>original: "agens."</note> and the passive, <note>original: "patiens."</note>
Between the husband and the wife, who remain of one mind. <note>A classic alchemical allegory where the "husband" usually represents Sulfur (the fixed, solar, or active principle) and the "wife" represents Mercury (the volatile, lunar, or passive principle).</note>

<summary>This page emphasizes the need for elemental separation before union and introduces the concept of the "middle way" in regulating moisture. It begins defining the three types of alchemical Conjunction, starting with the "double union" of active and passive principles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the instructions on "Conjunction" from the previous page, moving from the basic "double" union of the primary elements to the "triple" union of the philosophical trinity. Mercury, and living Sulfur: <note>original: "Mercurium, et Sulphur vivum." In the alchemical tradition, these are not the common chemical elements but the "philosophical" principles: Mercury represents the feminine, volatile, and fluid aspect, while Sulfur represents the masculine, fixed, and fiery aspect.</note> what remains is the subtle essence,
And Itself: <note>original: "Et Ipsum." Referring to the resulting unified substance which possesses both its physical and spiritual characteristics.</note> both form and matter at once.

Sufficiently instructed by these, you shall bind together the things to be united.
The second of the three <note>referring to the three types of conjunction mentioned on the previous page</note> is called the triple union
Of things, such as the Body, the Spirit, and the Soul. <note>original: "corpus, Spiritus, ac Anima." This alchemical trinity mimics the human composition: the Body is the physical salt or ash, the Spirit is the volatile oil or vapor that travels between states, and the Soul is the permanent essence or "tincture" that provides the color and life.</note>
These three are mixed until they are joined into one,
For this Trinity must be returned into its own Monad. <note>original: "Monadem." Derived from the Greek word for "unity" or "oneness," this refers to the ultimate goal of the Great Work—the creation of a single, indivisible, and perfect substance (the Philosopher's Stone).</note>

Thus knit the Soul to itself in everlasting peace.
Therefore, never cast these sayings to the careless breezes; <note>original: "securas dicta per auras." A common hermetic warning to keep the secrets of the art hidden from the uninitiated and to treat the instructions with solemnity.</note>
Let them be fixed firmly in your mind.

<meta>This page concludes the sequence on the different types of "Conjunction" (the joining of substances) described in the previous pages. Having moved from the double and triple unions, the author now addresses the final, most complex stage involving the four elements.</meta>

The third, which they propose, is called the **Quadruple Conjunction**: <note>original: "Coniunctio quadrupla." This is the final stage of union where all four classical elements—Earth, Air, Fire, and Water—are brought into a permanent, harmonious balance.</note>
It binds the four **Elements** together, so that they may remain fixed. <term>Elements: In alchemy, these are the four states of matter (Cold/Dry Earth, Hot/Moist Air, Hot/Dry Fire, and Cold/Moist Water) that must be balanced to create the Stone.</term>
Here you shall join the Elements, which were previously too scattered,
So that they may stand firm in a certain ratio to one another.
For just as the woman comes as three times five, <note>original: "ter uenit femina quinq." This likely refers to a proportion of 15 parts of the feminine, fluid "mercurial" principle.</note>
And the **Male** has five for his fertility: <note>original: "Mas... fertilitatis." The masculine principle, usually associated with "Sulfur," provides the "seed" or formative power to the substance.</note>
Not otherwise does our **Sun** contain only three parts of water, <term>Sun: Or "Sol," representing the "fixed" or fiery principle of the work, often symbolized as gold.</term>
While his spouse <note>The "Moon" or the feminine principle.</note> contains nine.
Yet these are but three, if I compare them to our Sun:
Thus the husband will always plow <note>original: "arit." From *arare*, to plow. In alchemical literature, "plowing" is a common metaphor for the labor of cultivation and the union of the substances in the "philosophical earth."</note> with his companion wife.
Let these words be sufficient; I have no need to write more,
And so it pleases me to bring this chapter to its conclusion.

However, I wish to give this warning: let the **Union** <note>original: "Coniunctio." In alchemy, this is the chemical "marriage" of substances, typically described as the union of the volatile "feminine" Mercury and the fixed "masculine" Sulfur.</note> be **Single**; the **Sacred Art** <term>Sacred Art: A common synonym for Alchemy, emphasizing its spiritual and guarded nature.</term> cannot bear more than one. 
For a harlot who makes a shameful profit from her body

and by being accustomed to adding raw materials to raw materials, 
the woman does not nourish what has been conceived, but loses it; she 
deceives herself, so that the growing **Work** <term>Work: Or "Opus," the entire alchemical process aimed at creating the Stone.</term> dies away.

**Vocabulary**

**Union** (Coniunctio), **Sacred Art** (Ars sacra), **Precious little stone** (preciosa lapilli), **Womb** (matrix), **Matter** (Materia), **The Work** (opus)

<meta>This page continues the journey through the "Twelve Gates" of alchemy. V

# ON PUTREFACTION <note>original: "DE PUTREFACTIONE." In alchemy, putrefaction is the stage where the matter is broken down, often turning black (the *nigredo* stage), to release its inner essence.</note>

> Now I shall relate what the true **putrefaction** <term>Putrefaction: The process of decay or decomposition that alchemists believed was necessary to "kill" the old form of a metal so a new, more perfect form could be born.</term> of the **Sages** <note>original: "Sophorum." The wise masters of the Hermetic art.</note> is,
> Without which there is no seed, nor will there be any harvest.
> This work is not a labor of hands upon the body; truly,
> It shall be the proper action of continuous heat.
> By **Nature**, bodies cannot be changed—with **Christ**
> as witness—unless they have first been made putrid. <note>This refers to the biblical principle found in John 12:24: "Unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone." Alchemists frequently used Christian resurrection as a metaphor for chemical processes.</note>
> Just as a grain of **wheat** never bears seed in the air,
> unless it dies enclosed in the fertile soil:
> So your material never has the new seeds of life,
> nor any powers, unless it undergoes putrefaction.

**Vocabulary of the Art:**
<note>The following terms appear in the Latin text as foundational concepts for this stage.</note>

**putrefaction** (decay/decomposition), **Sages** (the wise masters), **seed** (the potential within matter), **harvest** (the successful result of the work), **labor** (work), **heat** (the primary agent of change), **Nature**, **bodies** (the chemical substances), **wheat**, **ground** (the vessel or earth), **life**, **to be putrefied** (to undergo the process of decay).

<summary>This page describes Putrefaction as the essential Fifth Gate of alchemy, arguing that matter must "die" and decompose through steady heat before it can be transformed. It uses the biblical parable of the grain of wheat to explain that life and growth can only come from the breakdown of the original form.</summary>
<keywords>Putrefaction, Sages, Alchemical Heat, Nigredo, Grain of Wheat, Transformation, Fifth Gate, Spiritual Alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the Fifth Gate, Putrefaction. It follows the argument that material must die and be decomposed into its constituent elements before the alchemist can attempt to reunify them into the Philosopher's Stone. It emphasizes that this "death" is the prerequisite for all natural and supernatural generation.</meta>

Thus, the division of the **Elements** <note>original: "Elementorúm." The four traditional building blocks: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> will never be achieved,
And thus they cannot be perfectly joined.
Therefore, let **putrefaction** <term>Putrefaction: In alchemy, this is the "Black Work" or *nigredo*, where the matter is broken down into its most basic state through decay.</term> be known to you first, before
you approach the sacred work with empty hopes.
This is for the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophis." The practitioners of the Hermetic arts or alchemists.</note>: it is that which destroys the three bodies,
and divides our compound into three. <note>original: "corpora trina... tria." This refers to the *Tria Prima*—Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt—which the alchemist must separate from the base matter during this stage.</note>
This process causes these dead bodies to be corrupted; again,
it is the work of this same process that they may be reborn.
For whatever things you see being born into the **shores of light** <note>original: "in luminis oras." A poetic phrase meaning to be born or to come into the world of the living.</note>,
you see them generated by the celestial power from above.
From here, also wisely mixing the **Elements**, you shall bind them together,
so that the **heat** <note>original: "calor." Heat is the primary external agent used by the alchemist to facilitate the internal changes of the matter.</note> may ripen them with a gentle temperature.

**Core Vocabulary of the Art:**

| Original Term | English Translation | Definition |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Elementa** | Elements | The primary states of matter (Earth, Air, Fire, Water). |
| **Putrefaction** | Decay/Decomposition | The necessary "death" of the substance. |
| **Philosophis** | Alchemists | The "Sages" or "Philosophers" who understand Nature. |
| **Corpora** | Bodies | The physical substances or metals being transformed. |
| **Calor** | Heat | The fire or energy that drives the chemical process. |
| **Alchimia** | Alchemy | The sacred art of transformation. |

<summary>This page explains that putrefaction is the essential process that breaks a substance down into its three primary components—Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt—so they can be reborn. It stresses that no new life or "generation" can occur without this initial decay, guided by both celestial influence and controlled heat.</summary>

Indeed, the seeker should take care with watchful diligence, lest perhaps by some misstep 
these **waters** <note>original: "aquæ." In alchemy, the "waters" often refer to the mercurial solvent or the liquefied state of the matter being transformed.</note> are reduced into red ashes.

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the theme of the alchemical "marriage" and "gestation." It builds on the previous page's description of the furnace (Athanor) and the process of putrefaction, now framing the chemical reaction as a biological conception and a period of burial before rebirth.</meta>

If the moist elements, together with the hot, did not perform their duties
With constant motion <note>original: "motu continuo." In the alchemical vessel, this refers to the "circulation" of vapors—a process where the substance is heated, rises as steam, condenses, and falls back down in a continuous cycle.</note>, the entire work would perish:
The **matrix** <term>Matrix: From the Latin for "womb." In alchemy, this refers to the hermetic vessel (the glass flask or "philosopher's egg") where the chemical transformation is nurtured.</term> would not long retain the conceived **seeds** <term>Semina: The essential metallic principles or "tinctures" that the alchemist seeks to unite to create the Stone.</term>,
Nor would all living things bring forth their offspring.
In the beginning, therefore, understand our **stones** <note>original: "lapides nostros." Alchemists often referred to their initial materials as "stones," even when they were liquids or powders, to signify their hidden potential to become the final Philosopher's Stone.</note> in this way:
Let one be buried within the earth of the other.
Join them in an equal marriage; let them prepare the sacred
Rites of the shared bed; let them seek the flames and their union <note>original: "concubitus." Alchemical texts frequently use the metaphor of human reproduction—the "Chymical Wedding"—to describe the bonding of opposing chemicals like Sulfur (the hot/male) and Mercury (the moist/female).</note>:
Let them nourish the conceived seed; and let them provide, through that warmth,
The food in proportion to its needs <note>original: "victus pro ratione." This refers to the "regimen" of the fire; the alchemist must provide a specific amount of heat—neither too much nor too little—to "feed" the growing chemical reaction.</note>, so that it does not die.
At that time, let them not rise from the bottom of the **tomb** <term>Tumulus: A burial mound. In alchemy, the "tomb" is the bottom of the flask where the "blackness" (putrefaction) settles. The matter must remain "dead" at the bottom before it can "resurrect" and rise as a purified spirit.</term>;
This secret deceives many, and remains hidden.

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of human conception and burial to describe the "conjunction" of chemical elements, emphasizing that the substances must remain at the bottom of the vessel in a state of "death" or putrefaction to succeed.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemical Marriage, Matrix, Seeds of Metals, Putrefaction, Regimen of Heat, Chemical Wedding, Constant Motion</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical allegory of gestation. Having established the "marriage" of the elements on the previous page, the author now describes the physical signs of the first major stage of the Great Work—the *Nigredo* or blackening—using metaphors of pregnancy, decay, and the rainbow.</meta>

<sig>A</sig>

->The stages of conception must be awaited with this<-
->gentle heat: putrefaction will provide the signs of death.<-
<note>original: "mortis signa putredo dabit." In alchemy, the "death" of the raw materials through decay (putrefaction) was considered the necessary first step toward their rebirth as the Philosopher's Stone.</note>
->For at this very time, bubbles swell up<-
->in the manner of liquid snow being cast forth.<-
->And again they sink to the bottom of the vessel, and from there<-
->they waste away, until they are sufficiently putrefied.<-
<term>Putrefacta: Putrefaction. This is the first stage of the "Magnum Opus" (Great Work), where the substances are broken down into a black, fermented mass.</term>
->And just as when the rays of Phoebus are obstructed, the<-
<note>original: "Phœbi radijs." Phoebus is a name for the sun god, Apollo. In alchemical imagery, the Sun often represents gold or the "sulfuric" masculine principle.</note>
->multi-colored Iris stands decorated in her painted fold:<-
<term>Iris: The goddess of the rainbow. In alchemy, the "Peacock's Tail" (Cauda Pavonis) is a stage where many colors appear on the surface of the matter before it settles into a single color.</term>
->So there you see the shining colors vary themselves,<-
->when the liquid has begun to be dried out.<-
->Now, if a moderate moist heat acts within the<-
->bodies, it then makes these bodies black.<-
<note>original: "corpora nigra." This refers to the *Nigredo* stage, the literal blackening of the material which signaled that the "seeds" had successfully died and were ready to germinate.</note>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and allegorical description of the "Nigredo" or blackening phase. While the previous page described the shifting colors (the Peacock's Tail) and the onset of decay, this page focuses on the certainty of the black color as a sign of success and introduces the biblical allegory of Noah’s flood to explain the duration and drying of the alchemical matter.</meta>

For you, blackness is first the most certain sign,
That two things are joined, that two things are putrefied.
<note>original: "sint iuncta duo, sint putrefacta duo." The "two" refers to the masculine and feminine principles (often Sulfur and Mercury, or Sol and Luna) which must "die" together in the vessel to be reborn as one.</note>
It helps to remember these things, for then the variation of form,
Once known, does not allow you to wander into false paths.
Through this middle point, you enter the gate of true blackness,
If you wish to see the white signs of light.
<term>Candida signa: The "White Signs." This refers to the *Albedo*, the second major stage of the Great Work, which follows the blackness. The material is washed or purified until it turns a brilliant white.</term>
For Phoebus will first hide his lights at the rising,
<note>original: "Phœbus in exortu." Phoebus is the sun god. In this context, the "hiding of his light" refers to the golden or solar principle becoming hidden under the black crust of the *Nigredo*.</note>
And the flood of old Noah <gloss>22</gloss> will pass by.
<note>The number 22 likely refers to a specific chapter or citation in a symbolic commentary, or perhaps a duration of weeks.</note>
It persisted for this space of one hundred and fifty days,
<note>original: "centum, deciesq(ue) dierum / Quinto." The poet uses a complex phrasing for 150 days—the duration the waters of the Great Flood prevailed upon the earth in the Book of Genesis.</note>
Before the waters of the world first receded.
If our water, just as the songs of the wise say,
<term>Aqua nostra: "Our water." This is the "mercurial" solvent used by alchemists, which they claimed was not common water but a specialized substance capable of dissolving metals.</term>
Passes away, and our Rivers depart into dryness.
<note>This refers to the "coagulation" or "fixation" of the matter. After the "flood" (the liquid stage), the moisture must be evaporated or absorbed until the matter becomes a dry, solid powder.</note>

<sig>G</sig>
<meta>Modern library watermark overlay: "RESERVED © Biblioteca Apostolica Vaticana". Catchword or partial text from previous page visible on far left margin.</meta>

<summary>This page identifies the "Nigredo" (blackness) as the definitive proof of successful putrefaction and the union of alchemical principles. It uses the 150-day duration of Noah’s flood as an allegory for the time the matter remains in a liquid state before drying into a solid.</summary>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page follows the description of the Great Flood and the drying of the alchemical matter (the Nigredo). Having survived the "rains" or the volatile liquid state, the practitioner is now promised the first signs of growth and the eventual appearance of the red color, marking the transition toward the final stages of the work.</meta>

Storing these things in a receptive mind, keep them deep within your heart,
Lest a straying error lead you astray alongside the foolish.
Soon after, the Vineyard of the Patriarch Noah is planted,
<note>original: "Vinea Noæ." In alchemical allegory, Noah’s vineyard represents the stage of renewal and growth that follows the "Flood" (the purification and blackening of the matter).</note>
Which immediately flourishes, turning green and filled with welcome flowers.
Once these things are achieved, you need not fear the dangers of rain.
For you will see the first sprouts of your **Stone**.
<term>Lapidis: The Philosopher's Stone. The author uses the metaphor of a germinating plant to describe the "life" returning to the chemically purified material.</term>
And when the Sun <note>original: "Phœbus"</note> has completed thirty circuits,
One stage will appear to you full of a deep red color.
I call this "red lead"; <note>original: "plumbum rubeum." Likely referring to the *Rubedo*, the final red stage of the alchemical process, rather than common lead oxide.</note> I also name it "**Sulfur**" by this <unclear>name</unclear>;
Lest the foolish person reaps my harvest with his sickle.
<note>The author admits to using "Decknames"—cryptic terminology intended to hide the true ingredients or processes from those who are not initiated into the art.</note>
Now, just as the **Soul** <note>original: "Anima"</note> is released from the ship and from its sufferings,
They seek the Blessed **Elysium** where life is found:
<note>original: "Elysium Felix." In classical mythology, the final resting place of the heroic and virtuous. Here, it represents the state of perfection and stability reached by the alchemical matter after the "punishments" of the fire and the "shipwreck" of the dissolution.</note>

<summary>This page describes the transition from the purification stage to the initial growth of the Philosopher's Stone, using the metaphor of Noah's vineyard. The author introduces the onset of the red stage (Rubedo) and explains that technical terms like "red lead" are used as veils to protect the secret from the unworthy.</summary>

<keywords>Vineyard of Noah, Philosopher's Stone, Phoebus, Red Lead, Sulfur, Rubedo, Elysium, alchemical allegory, decknames</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical journey of the alchemical matter. Following the previous page's mention of the "Blessed Elysium," the text now describes the emergence of the Stone from its dark, chaotic state (the Nigredo) into a perfected light. It concludes with a practical warning regarding the process of "putrefaction," using the metaphor of an oak tree to explain why the matter must not be overly submerged if it is to break down properly.</meta>

Thus when the **Stone** is made pure from the thick darkness,
<term>Stone: The *Lapis Philosophorum* or Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical work.</term>
And the **Elements** <note>original: "Elementa." The four classical elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> are silent, bound together in peace:
On account of the most beautiful gifts of supreme form from their unions,
<note>original: "Coniugiis." Often referred to as the "Chemical Wedding," this is the successful joining of opposites (like Sulfur and Mercury) within the vessel.</note>
It will perceive true joys in its silent breast.
And the light will pass from the dark gloom of the **Styx**
<note>original: "Stygis." In Greek mythology, the Styx is the river of the underworld. Here, it represents the dark, "dead" stage of the matter before its transformation.</note>
To the light of **Paradise**: thus also will it be the **Elixir**;
<term>Elixir: A substance capable of prolonging life or transmuting metals, representing the Stone in its medicinal or liquid form.</term>
But so that the **putrefaction** of your Stone may happen more quickly,
<term>Putrefactio: A vital stage in alchemy where the matter must "die" and rot to be reborn. Without this breakdown, the transformation cannot proceed.</term>
Take this example, which may be of benefit to you:
If an **oak tree** <note>original: "quercús"</note> should be soaked in a perennial whirlpool,
It will not rot quickly, believe me.
For if it were kept under the waters for a hundred years,
Or even more, it will always remain whole!
<note>The author provides a practical warning: just as an oak submerged entirely in deep water resists decay, the alchemical matter will not undergo the necessary "putrefaction" if it is drowned in too much liquid (the "mercurial water"). Success requires a balance of moisture and heat.</note>

<summary>This page describes the transition of the alchemical matter from the darkness of the "Styx" to the light of "Paradise," marking its transformation into the Elixir. It also warns the practitioner that excessive liquid prevents the necessary stage of putrefaction, comparing it to an oak tree that remains preserved when submerged.</summary>

<keywords>Philosopher's Stone, Nigredo, Putrefaction, Elixir, Four Elements, Alchemical Marriage, Oak Tree metaphor, Styx, Paradise</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for "putrefaction" (the breakdown of matter). While the previous page established that constant submersion prevents rotting, this page explains that the secret to the process lies in the alternation of opposing states—dryness and moisture, heat and cold—to ensure the "seeds" of the work can sprout.</meta>

But if it is dry at one time, and then wet again, 
In the course of time it will become **rotten** <note>original: "putrìs."</note>.
Thus if **Our Tree** <term>Arbor Nostra: A symbolic name for the alchemical substance as it "grows" and transforms within the vessel.</term> is scorched now by the rays of **Phoebus** <note>original: "Phoebi." The sun-god, representing the element of Fire or external heat applied to the flask.</note>, 
And immediately grows wet again with water:
The seeds will rot by no difficult path, 
So that soon they may push forth a bud from a fertile branch.
For if the matter is dry now, and then wet again; 
If it has heat now, and then cold,
Then it undergoes **putrefaction** <note>original: "putrescit." The essential stage of decay before rebirth.</note>. 
From this, your **Sun** <note>original: "Sol." In this context, it refers to the "philosophical gold" or the central masculine principle of the work.</note> itself will soon rot. 
You should treat our bodies in this way.
See that you finish the work for me, and only with a nourishing **heat** <note>original: "calore." Usually a gentle, "incubating" heat like that of a brooding hen.</note>, 
Lest your gains perhaps fall into **ashes** <note>original: "cineres." The scorched, useless remains of a failed experiment where the fire was too strong.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the method of achieving alchemical putrefaction by alternating the matter between dry/hot and wet/cold states. This "stressing" of the substance allows the "Arbor Nostra" to break down so that new life (the Stone) can eventually germinate.</summary>
<keywords>putrefaction, Arbor Nostra, Phoebus, Sol, alchemical heat, decomposition, rebirth, ashes</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>Following the previous page’s discussion on the necessity of alternating dryness and moisture to achieve putrefaction, this page provides specific instructions on the "circular" process of distillation and condensation. It warns the practitioner against the dangers of excessive heat and clarifies the true nature of the materials required for the Great Work.</meta>

Extract the **water** <note>original: "limpham." Used here to describe the clear, volatile liquid or "spirit" extracted from the solid matter.</note> from the earth: once lifted through the heights,
Let the **animated part** <note>original: "pars animata." The vital essence or "soul" of the substance that is released during heating.</note> seek the heavens along with the water:
Press down again that which was raised: and let them sink as many times,
As they ascend to the high roofs of the **pole** <note>original: "poli." A poetic reference to the celestial heavens, representing the top of the alchemical vessel.</note>.
You must protect the hot **glass** <note>original: "vitrum." Referring to the "Philosophical Egg" or the flask containing the work.</note> from sudden cold;
Ensure that the external heat does not act with violence.
Use a gentle **fire** <note>original: "igne." Alchemists prioritized a slow, steady heat—often likened to the warmth of a nesting bird—to nurture the transformation.</note>, one which causes no harm,
Lest your material become a fused **mass of glass** <note>original: "massa vitri." This refers to vitrification, a failure where the materials melt into a useless, stony slag due to excessive heat.</note>.
Know the matter that is suited for things being born;
Choose neither common Salt, nor **Sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur." The author distinguishes between the common yellow sulfur of the druggist and the "Philosophical Sulfur" needed for alchemy.</note>, nor any Mineral.
For **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius."</note> and my **Sulfur** <note>original: "Sulphur."</note> lie hidden, enclosed within the metals,
No matter what you babble, you foolish **Sophist** <term>Sophista: A derogatory term for "false" alchemists who follow recipes literally without understanding the underlying natural philosophy.</term>.

<summary>This page details the process of "circular distillation," where the essence of the matter is repeatedly evaporated and condensed to purify it. It emphasizes the need for a gentle heat and warns that the true Mercury and Sulfur of the Wise are found hidden within metals, rather than in common minerals.</summary>
<keywords>distillation, circulation, gentle fire, vitrification, philosophical sulfur, philosophical mercury, sophist, animated part</keywords>

These two they often call "oil," as well as "birds" and "beasts"; <note>The "two" refers to the Mercury and Sulfur mentioned on the previous page. Alchemists used "birds" to represent volatile substances that evaporate and "beasts" to represent fixed substances that resist fire.</note>

->That you may avoid the false and know the true words,<-
You will be wealthy, having few equals in **riches** <note>original: "Diuitijs."</note>,
->And you will be rich in the weight of **gold** <note>original: "auri."</note>, if you so desire.<-

**Stone**, **Sages**, **Nature**, **Gold**, **Cement of the World**, **wealthy**, **Riches**

<summary>This page explains that the true ingredients of alchemy are hidden behind animal allegories to keep them secret from the "foolish." It describes the Philosopher's Stone as the fundamental "cement" of the universe and promises immense spiritual and material wealth to those who successfully multiply it.</summary>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 49. While the previous page ended by describing the success and riches awaiting the true philosopher who follows Nature, this page shifts to a cautionary description of the "false" alchemists who fail in their work and suffer physical ruin.</meta>

But you see many others proceeding foolishly in their work,
For whom there is a **Tincture** <note>original: "Tinctura." In alchemy, the tincture is the elusive "medicine" or substance capable of transmuting and coloring base metals into gold or silver.</note> of beautiful color.
They make it red or white with their hands; but while that tincture
Flees from the flames, it is consumed by the fire and perishes. <note>This refers to substances that are volatile; they change color but evaporate or burn away because they have not been "fixed" to withstand heat.</note>
These I would call the **breakers of vessels** <note>original: "vasorum ruptores." A derogatory term for "puffers" or clumsy practitioners who used excessive heat, causing their glass laboratory equipment to shatter.</note>: who
Kill their very selves while they handle dreadful poisons <note>original: "toxica dira." Alchemical experiments often involved dangerous materials like mercury, arsenic, and lead, which produced lethal fumes.</note>.
And while they drag out sleepless nights in such great peril,
They cloud their eyes with heavy, thick smoke.
You may see them walking about foul in tattered clothes,
And whatever path they take, they reek of **sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur." One of the three primary principles of alchemy, known for its pungent "rotten egg" smell when burned.</note>:
**Corrosives** <term>Corrosiva|Strong acids or alkaline substances that eat away at materials and skin.</term> stain their hands; bleary <note>original: "lippi oculi." This suggests chronic inflammation or "sore eyes" caused by constant exposure to chemical irritants.</note>
Are their eyes with dimness; and from the smoke, their cheeks grow thin <note>original: "macilenta gena." A description of the haggard, wasted appearance of the failed alchemist.</note>.

<summary>This page uses vivid imagery to describe the "breakers of vessels," failed alchemists whose poor methods lead to poverty, physical illness, and the destruction of their materials.</summary>
